<meta>This entry describes the exterior binding of the manuscript. As the first physical encounter with the volume, it provides context for the book's age and craftsmanship before the internal text begins.</meta>

# Front Cover and Spine

The volume is bound in a historical <term>mottled calfskin</term> <note>a type of leather treated with acid to create a decorative, marbled, or spotted effect</note> of a brown hue. The front cover is embellished with a decorative border consisting of three parallel lines in <term>gilt-tooling</term> <note>a technique where gold leaf is pressed into the leather using heated brass stamps or wheels</note>. 

The leather exhibits significant signs of age and use, including scuffing, staining, and small patches where the surface of the leather has been lost over the centuries.

### Spine

To the left, the spine of the volume is visible, characterized by <term>raised bands</term> <note>the horizontal ridges created by the internal cords used to sew the book's sections together</note>. Several red leather labels are affixed between these bands, though the gold-stamped lettering is now fragmentary and worn.

**THE...** <note>original French: "LES." This is the only clearly legible word remaining on the uppermost spine label; the rest of the title has been lost to time.</note>

<unclear>fragmentary lettering</unclear>

<summary>The exterior of the volume features a classic mottled leather binding with gold-tooled borders and raised bands on the spine. The only surviving legible text on the spine is the French definite article "LES" (The).</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, mottled calfskin, gilt-tooling, spine labels, raised bands, 18th-century binding, French title fragment</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the exterior binding to the interior of the front cover. It focuses on the personal markings left by a former owner, providing evidence of the manuscript's provenance and history of ownership.</meta>

The interior of the front cover features a vibrant marbled paper <term>pastedown</term> <note>the sheet of paper glued to the inner surface of the book's cover to hide the raw edges of the binding material</note>. The paper displays a complex "shell" or "feather" pattern in shades of red, yellow, green, and blue. There is slight wear and surface loss on the left edge of the paper.

In the center of the marbled paper is a rectangular white <term>bookplate</term> <note>also known as an "ex-libris," a decorative label indicating the book's owner</note>. The plate features an ornate frame surrounding an illustration of a book viewed through a set of iron bars. The inner border of the book's binding, decorated with a gold-tooled floral pattern, is also visible.

<meta>This is the title page of the first edition of Gabriel Naudé’s "Instruction à la France," published in 1623. This work is a seminal text in 17th-century French rationalism, written to debunk the mysterious Rosicrucian "furore" that was then sweeping through Paris.</meta>

## INSTRUCTION
# TO FRANCE
### ON THE TRUTH
### OF THE HISTORY OF THE
### BROTHERS OF THE
## Rose-Cross.

->By G. <term>NAUDÉ</term> Parisian.<-
<note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653) was a famous French scholar and librarian. In this work, he took a skeptical, rationalist approach to the supernatural rumors of his day.</note>

->*Oh, how much emptiness there is in human affairs.* <note>original Latin: "O quantum est in rebus inane."</note> Persius, Satires 1.<-

<image-desc>A circular printer's device featuring a radiant sun with a human face. Above the sun, a scroll contains the motto "AFTER THE CLOUDS, THE SUN" <note>original Latin: "POST NVBILA PHOEBVS"</note>. The sun is encircled by an ornate wreath of fruit and flowers. At the bottom of the device is a small medallion containing the initials "FI" (for the publisher François Julliot) surmounted by a cross.</image-desc>

->IN PARIS,<-
->At the shop of FRANÇOIS JULLIOT, at the third<-
->pillar of the Great Hall of the Palace.<-
<note>The Great Hall (Grand' Salle) of the Palais de Justice in Paris was a famous commercial hub where booksellers and other merchants rented space around the stone pillars.</note>

->1623.<-

->WITH THE PRIVILEGE OF THE KING.<-
<note>A "Privilege" was a legal permit from the King that gave a publisher the exclusive right to print a specific book for a set number of years, acting as an early form of copyright.</note>

<summary>This title page introduces Gabriel Naudé’s critical investigation into the Rosicrucian phenomenon, a mysterious secret society that caused a public sensation in 1623. The choice of a quote from the satirist Persius immediately signals the author's skeptical view of the movement as an "empty" vanity.</summary>

# TO THE NOBLE LORD
## GABRIEL DE GUÉNÉGAUD
->Lord of the aforementioned place and of Plessis-Belleville,<-
->Councilor and Secretary to the King<-
->in his Councils of State and Finance.<-

Sir,

As my intention in this <term>Instruction<note>A 17th-century term for a treatise or educational manual designed to provide guidance on a specific subject.</note></term> has never been anything other than to set against the palpable darkness of falsehood the sun of truth, which, through the rays of its light, made the most noble part of our <term>Hemisphere<note>Original: "Hemisphere." Here, the author likely refers to Europe or the "known world" of the Western intellectual and religious tradition.</note></term> recognize the perilous labyrinth in which it had become entangled during its absence:

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication to Gabriel de Guénégaud, a powerful French financier and state official, framing the subsequent text as a work of "truth" intended to lead the reader out of intellectual or spiritual "darkness."</summary>
<keywords>Gabriel de Guénégaud, Plessis-Belleville, dedication, Enlightenment, truth vs. falsehood, 17th-century French administration</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication (Epistle) to Gabriel de Guénégaud. The author transitions from describing the "darkness of falsehood" on the previous page to explaining his own humble intentions in addressing such a high-ranking patron, distancing himself from the hollow flattery common in contemporary literature.</meta>

# EPISTRE.

So it is also necessary that, in order not to imitate in this <term>Epistle<note>A formal letter, common in the 17th century as a vehicle for dedicating a book to a powerful patron.</note></term> the vanity of certain spirits of this age, who believe they are consecrating to posterity the memory of countless individuals whose names they engrave with their pens upon the marble of their writings: I honestly confess that such presumption has not held such power over me that—by allowing my ignorance to take flight far above the limits of my ability—it could persuade me that this little Book should present itself to the starry heaven of your merits, adorned with such effrontery as to hope it could increase your light with the torch and tiny sparks of my <term>conceptions<note>The author's original thoughts, ideas, or the intellectual substance of the book.</note></term>.

That is why I prefer to acknowledge and openly confess that, among an infinity of other reasons, these two principal ones have given me—

<meta>catchword: born [French: né...]</meta>

<summary>In this portion of the dedication, the author humbly disavows the "vanity" of other writers who use flattery to seek fame, claiming his own "small book" is merely a collection of "tiny sparks" compared to the brilliance of his patron's merits.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, humility, 17th-century literature, patronage, Gabriel de Guénégaud, intellectual modesty</keywords>

# EPISTRE.

<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to the patron. The author transitions from praising the patron's status to explaining his own humble motivations for publishing this work, contrasting himself with more boastful writers of the 17th century.</meta>

So it must be that, to avoid imitating in this **Epistle** <note>A formal letter, typically used as a dedication in 17th-century books to seek the protection of a powerful patron.</note> the vanity of certain spirits of this age, who think to consecrate to **posterity** <note>Future generations or the permanent historical record.</note> the memory of an infinite number of people—whose names they engrave with their pens upon the marble of their writings—I ingenuously confess that **presumption** <note>Audacity or excessive pride.</note> has not had such power over me. A metaphor comparing the patron’s virtues to the vastness and brilliance of the heavens.</note> of your merits, adorned with such effrontery as to hope to increase your light with the **torch** and small sparks of my **conceptions** <note>The author’s original ideas or intellectual efforts.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to the Patron (G. Naudé’s "Epistle") to the "Preface to the Reader." The previous page ended with Naudé's signature and a closing of service. ## TO THE READER.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'I' set within a square frame. The letter is flanked by two nude male figures supporting it, surrounded by foliate and architectural elements.</image-desc>

It is true, dear Reader, and I confess it to you frankly, that I had never yet had such a conflict within myself; and similar extremes had never enveloped me in such great uncertainty as that in which I found myself reduced and entangled a few days ago. Nature obliged me on one hand to sympathize with the afflictions and give some relief to the extreme exhaustion <note>The author uses "langueurs" to describe the state of weakness following a crisis.</note> of this city, once so flourishing, recently beaten and afflicted by the plague-ridden arrows of a contagious disease <note>This likely refers to the plague outbreaks that frequently struck Paris in the early 17th century.</note>; and now all stirred up and panting from the venom of a false and ridiculous opinion, which some useless drones and fools <term>buses<gloss>Literally "buzzards," a bird of prey considered difficult to train, used here as a metaphor for stupid or ignorant people.</gloss></term> of the human race strive to slip imperceptibly into the imagination of its dearest children <note>Original: "nourriçons," meaning the city’s inhabitants or intellectual "nurslings."</note>, to shake their judgment and blunt the good opinion that one had of their prudence. And ignorance, on the other hand, counteracting the designs of this good will, would not allow me to gather from the young grafts and new plants

<meta>catchword: of my</meta>

<summary>In this preface, Gabriel Naudé addresses the reader to explain his inner conflict between the desire to help a city reeling from both the plague and "ridiculous" false rumors, and the difficulties posed by the ignorance of those spreading them.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Gabriel Naudé, public opinion, plague, ignorance, 17th-century Paris, rumors</keywords>

This page resolves that tension as the author finds inspiration in Seneca to write despite his doubts.</meta>

This is why I was in total despair of being able to offer any testimony of my affection for France, until, to free myself from all these anxieties and indecisions, I turned to this judgment of Seneca: *A good mind needs but little learning* <note>original: "Paucis ad bonam mentem opus est literis." Seneca suggests that wisdom and virtue are more important than vast, exhaustive academic study.</note>. I can say that it is truly he who gave me the boldness, through the truth of his maxim, to set hand to pen, to unseal the eyes of your understanding, to strike down the films and cataracts of falsehood, and to make you recognize the truth that you must follow to instruct yourselves in the history of the Rosicrucians (as Master Boucher calls them in his *Mystic Crown* <note>Jean Boucher (c. 1548–1644) was a prominent theologian; his "Couronne mystique" (1623) was a work of religious polemic.</note>) or Brothers of the Rose-Cross.

# TO THE READER.

And ignorance, on the other hand, counterbalancing the designs of this good will, would not permit me to gather from the young grafts and new plants of my

<summary>The author describes his internal struggle between wanting to defend Paris from the plague and the "venomous" Rosicrucian rumors, and his own feelings of intellectual inadequacy. He characterizes those spreading the rumors as ignorant and useless members of society.</summary>

This is why I utterly despaired of being able to give some testimony of my affection to *France*, until, to deliver myself from all these anxieties and irresolutions, I relied upon this judgment of *Seneca*: *"To achieve a sound mind, few books are needed"* <note>original Latin: "Paucis ad bonam mentem opus est literis." --- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader." The author is currently critiquing the interpretations of the "Rose-Cross" name, specifically addressing an argument (likely by Father Garasse mentioned on the previous page) that links the symbol to the concept of silence and secrecy.</meta>

as the sole soul and primary principle of all his actions: in proof of which interpretation he fortifies himself with the last two lines of an epigram <note>An epigram is a brief, clever, and often satirical poem.</note>, which are explained so naturally <note>original: "naïfuement" — in this context, it means simply or straightforwardly, without artifice.</note> by the first two (which he omitted), that I judged there was no need for any other commentary than to present them to you in their full and perfect sense:

> *The rose is Venus’ flower; to hide her secret loves,*
> *Cupid gave his mother’s gift to Harpocrates.* <note>Harpocrates was the Greek adaptation of the Egyptian god Horus, often depicted with a finger to his lips as the god of silence and secrets.</note>
> *Hence the host hangs a rose over friendly tables,*
> *So guests may know that things said under it are to remain silent.* <note>original Latin: "Est rosa flos Veneris, cuius quo furta laterent, / Harpocrati, matris, dona dicauit Amor, / Inde rosam mensis hospes suspendit amicis, / Conuiua ut sub ea dicta, tacenda sciant." This famous poem explains the origin of the phrase *sub rosa* (under the rose), signifying confidentiality.</note>

Some others have striven to explain the mystery hidden beneath this Cross of Roses <term>Croix de Rozes: The Rose Cross, the central symbol of the Rosicrucian Order, which the author is attempting to demystify.</term> by other reasons and various conjectures. Following the light now revealed here, we ought to value these no more than the writings of all the Germans <note>original: "Allemans" — a reference to the fact that the Rosicrucian movement originated in Germany with the publication of the *Fama Fraternitatis* in 1614.</note> who have given free rein to their imaginations on this subject. They have debated the pros and cons of this Society <note>The "Society" refers to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood.</note>, and though they are more numerous than one could imagine, they have nevertheless handled the argument in such a way that they seem more intent on piling up many vain and useless words to fatten their volumes than on selecting the most beautiful and necessary points to satisfy and content the curiosity of the reader. And to prove this is so, I make a judge of the first among those who have had the patience to

<summary>The author explains the symbol of the rose as a classical sign of silence (sub rosa) and dismisses previous German writings on the Rosicrucians as long-winded and imaginative fantasies.</summary>

# To the Reader.

Reader, the cross joined to the rose, and the brothers' proud
    claims <note>original Latin: "thesis."</note> have often left you in a state of uncertainty;
Come here, and you shall be permitted to know the hollow
    outcomes of such great folly, and the extent of the error within.
Take NAUDÉ <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), the author of this work and a prominent French scholar and librarian known for his skeptical approach to magic and secret societies.</note> as your guide; read, and read thoroughly, for
    to read through NAUDÉ’S book is the same thing as being wise.

->I. L. D. D. M.<-

<meta>This page continues the author’s preface ("To the Reader"), where Gabriel Naudé justifies the concise nature of his work and explains the haste of its composition. # TO THE READER.

...several other chapters against an infinity of fantasies similar to this one: to finally conclude in the negative, and show how much all these fabulous narratives have always been dangerous and prejudicial to those who have nurtured and fomented them. This is, in my judgment, everything one can say or desire on such a matter, shortened (like a **Giant** in the bezel of a ring, or this great world on a small globe) within the ten chapters of this present **Instruction**, which should be all the more agreeable to you as it gives you—in seven or eight sheets of paper, and by a very easy method—that which you could only draw with great difficulty from reading all those who wrote on this subject before me. Furthermore, **Celsus** <note>Aulus Cornelius Celsus (c. 25 BC – c. 50 AD), a Roman writer whose medical treatise *De Medicina* was a foundational text in the Renaissance.</note> advises you in my favor: *although their knowledge may be equal, a physician who is a friend is more useful than a stranger* <note>original Latin: "cum par scientia sit, tamen utiliorem Medicum esse amicum quàm extraneum."</note>. It is quite true that I could have presented it to you more accomplished and perfected, if the remedy (which relied on promptness) had permitted me to spend more than fifteen days on its composition, and to watch more diligently for the faults that occurred during its printing. These errors, being few in number, do not deserve to be separated from the end of this **notice** <note>original French: "aduertissement," referring to the preface/foreword.</note>. This is why I beg you, in reward for this small labor I have undertaken...

<summary>In this concluding portion of the preface, Gabriel Naudé highlights the brevity and efficiency of his "Instruction," claiming to have condensed a vast subject into a short, accessible format. He defends the speed of his writing—just fifteen days—and uses a medical analogy from Celsus to position himself as a helpful "friend" to the reader.</summary>

<keywords>Gabriel Naudé, Celsus, Rosicrucians, brevity, skepticism, 17th-century printing, polemics</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the administrative and technical front matter (errata and the Latin address to the reader) into the formal commendatory section. # TO MONSIEUR NAUDÉ
## ON HIS BOOK AGAINST
### the Brothers of the Rose-Cross,
### S O N N E T.

*You who, following the error of your imagination,*
*And cloaking that pride with which you are possessed,*
*Slip your poison into those weak minds*
*Who suspect nothing of your hypocrisy:*
*You shall no longer deceive Europe nor Asia,*
*Your mask is removed, and you are held in contempt,*
*Ever since this Author, in his learned writings,*
*Reveals the blindness with which your soul is gripped.*
*Persevere then, noble Spirit, and purge this universe,*
*Like Hercules of old, of these diverse monsters,* <note>The author compares Naudé to Hercules performing his Labors, specifically the cleansing of the world from mythical monsters.</note>
*Who produce such harmful effects everywhere:*
*Apollo deceives me, or I am certain,* <note>Apollo was the god of truth, prophecy, and healing; the poet suggests that by divine inspiration, he knows Naudé will succeed.</note>
*That the more you confound these invisible Doctors,* <note>The Rosicrucians were famously called "The Invisibles" in 17th-century Paris because they claimed to be a secret society that could not be seen by the uninitiated.</note>
*The more visible you shall be, and the more greatly admired.*

G. C. P. A. <note>These initials likely identify a friend or fellow scholar of Naudé, though his exact identity remains a matter of bibliographical debate.</note>

<summary>A commendatory sonnet praising Gabriel Naudé for exposing the Rosicrucian "Invisibles" as hypocrites and frauds. The poem uses classical imagery, comparing Naudé’s intellectual work to the heroic labors of Hercules.</summary>
<keywords>Gabriel Naudé, Rosicrucians, Sonnet, Counter-Reformation, Hercules, Apollo, The Invisibles, 17th-century polemic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of commendatory verses dedicated to Gabriel Naudé. # TO MONSIEUR NAUDÉ
## ON HIS *INSTRUCTION* TO FRANCE. <note>Referring to the title of Naudé’s book, *Instruction à la France sur la vérité de l'histoire des Frères de la Roze-Croix* (Instruction to France on the Truth of the History of the Brothers of the Rose-Cross), published in 1623.</note>
### STANZAS.

*Those men, or those demi-gods,*
*According to their ambitious title,*
*Who join the Cross to the Rose,* <note>The Rosicrucians, or "Brothers of the Rose-Cross," were a mysterious secret society that claimed to possess ancient esoteric wisdom.</note>
*If it be true what is said of their profound knowledge,*
*Naudé, my spirit is inclined*
*To follow their Genius, and to do as they do.* <note>The poet suggests, perhaps ironically, that if their claims were true, he would gladly follow their guiding spirit.</note>

*They steal away so subtly*
*Their bodies from our perception,*
*According to the reports of the common folk,*
*That he who would not admire the effect,*
*His soul, so dull and clumsy,*
*Would make me suspect that Medusa had fashioned it.* <note>In Greek mythology, the gaze of the Gorgon Medusa turned people to stone; the poet suggests that anyone unmoved by the Rosicrucians' "invisibility" must be as unfeeling as a statue.</note>

*Our soul, once freed from error,*
*And our bodies exempt from the horror*
*That the grave brings forth,*
*Would taste with them infinite pleasures;*
*By refusing this blessed state,*
*Either Heaven does us wrong, or we shall be punished.* <note>This stanza reflects on the Rosicrucian claim of physical immortality. The poet argues that if such a state were possible, refusing it would be a tragedy or a divine punishment.</note>

<summary>A poem composed of three stanzas that reflects on the supernatural claims of the Rosicrucians, specifically their alleged invisibility and immortality. The author uses classical allusions to Medusa to emphasize the wonder—or the gullibility—associated with these mysterious figures.</summary>
<keywords>Gabriel Naudé, Rosicrucians, Rose-Cross, invisibility, Medusa, immortality, 17th-century poetry, occultism</keywords>

*But a rumor that spreads without an author,*
*I suspect it of being a liar;*
*And the strokes of your eloquence,*
*In the sight of which I soothe my anxiety,*
*So firmly bind my faith,*
*That if you believe nothing of it, I believe nothing either.* <note>Gaudé concludes by placing his total intellectual trust in Naudé; if the scholar finds no truth in the Rosicrucian claims, the poet will follow suit.</note>

*Ah! How charming is your discourse!*
*It imperceptibly steals away,*
*Through an uncommon ecstasy,*
*My speech and my senses; I wish still to court*
*Minerva,* <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom, medicine, and the arts.</note> *and my own good fortune,*
*So that one day, if possible, I might imitate you.*

<meta>This page contains the "Privilege du Roy," or Royal Privilege, which was the official legal authorization required to publish a book in 17th-century France. This document follows the Table of Contents and grants Gabriel Naudé the exclusive right to print his critique of the Rosicrucians.</meta>

### Royal Privilege. <note>original: "Privilege du Roy." This was a form of copyright and censorship clearance granted by the crown.</note>

LOUIS, by the grace of God King of France and Navarre, To our beloved and loyal Councilors, the members of our Courts of Parliament, the Masters of Requests <note>High-ranking judicial officers in the king's household.</note> of our Palace, the Provost of Paris, and to all our other Justices and Officers to whom this shall concern, Greetings. Our well-beloved Gabriel Naudé has informed and represented to us that he has composed a book entitled, *Instruction to France on the Truth of the History of the Brothers of the Rose Cross* <note>original: "Freres de la Roze-Croix." Naudé’s work was a skeptical response to the sudden appearance of Rosicrucian manifestos in Paris in 1623.</note>, which he desires to have printed and published. However, he fears that others, besides the person he has charged with this task, might have it printed if he were not provided with our suitable Letters for this purpose, for which he humbly petitions. **FOR THESE REASONS**, being liberally inclined toward the request of the said Naudé; and after it has appeared to us that he has obtained the approval of two Doctors of the Faculty of Theology of the University of our good city of Paris: We have, by our special grace, full power, and Royal authority, given and granted, and by these Presents do give and grant, leave and permission to have the aforementioned Book printed in such characters <note>Referring to typefaces or fonts.</note> and by such merchant Printer and Bookseller as he shall see fit, for the time and space of six years, beginning from the day the printing is completed. We forbid all Printers, Booksellers, and others of whatever quality and condition they may be from printing or having it printed and offered for sale during the said time, without the leave and permission of the said petitioner, under penalty of a fine of five hundred livres, with half applicable to the confined poor <note>original: "pauures enfermez," referring to those in state-run hospitals or almshouses.</note> and the other half to the person he has commissioned to print the said Book, along with all costs, damages, and interests. On the condition of placing two copies in our Library of the Cordeliers <note>The library of the Franciscan friars in Paris, which served as a royal depository for books at this time.</note>. To this end, we give you power and special mandate. Notwithstanding any oppositions or appeals whatsoever, for which—and without prejudice to them—we do not wish this to be delayed. We further desire that by placing these Presents, or an extract thereof, at the beginning or end of the said Book, they shall be considered as officially served.

<summary>This page presents the formal royal decree granting Gabriel Naudé the exclusive rights to publish his book on the Rosicrucians for six years, establishing legal protection against piracy and confirming theological approval.</summary>
<keywords>Royal Privilege, Gabriel Naudé, Rosicrucians, publishing laws, 17th-century France, King Louis XIII, book licensing</keywords>

**FOR SUCH** is our pleasure. Given at Paris the thirteenth day of November, the year of grace one thousand six hundred twenty-three: and of our reign the fourteenth.

->By the Council, PETIT.<-

*The said Naudé has granted to François Julliot <note>The publisher/bookseller who received the commercial rights from the author.</note> that he may enjoy the contents of the Privilege above, on the first day of December 1623.*

# F. G. MENAPIUS ON
## THE EPIGRAMS OF THE BROTHERS OF THE ROSE CROSS <note>original: "F. R. C." stands for Fratres Rosae Crucis. This poem is a direct attack on the Rosicrucians' perceived arrogance.</note>
### Sent in the place of a New Year's gift <note>original: "Strenæ loco." A *strena* was a traditional gift, usually given on the first of January, intended as a good omen.</note>.

> *Those who assist with excellent work*
> *The cultivators of good things, and those eager for letters,*
> *Among whom shines like a fire-gem* <note>original: "Pyropus," a fiery red gemstone or a gold-bronze alloy mentioned by ancient writers like Ovid.</note>
> *Among jewels and Indian stones,*
> GALEN <note>original: "GALENVS." The 2nd-century Greek physician whose works were the foundation of traditional medicine. Naudé and his circle were defending Galenic medicine against the mystical alchemy of the Rosicrucians.</note>, *the Glory of all Schools,*
> *Who surpasses all, as many as either have been,*
> *Or are, or will be in other years:*
> *All these you wickedly scorn in your little books.*
> *Let the Father of refinements,*
> *And the fountain of all erudition*
> *Live famously in the world, let him live on,*
> *Let him live, and flourish, let him be read forever,*
> *Let him be praised, let him please: let him be read, and*
> *Let him be loved: Let the Father of Erudition*
> *Live; may you perish here and everywhere.*

Whoever does not trust Galen <note>original: "Galeno." Galen of Pergamon (2nd century AD) was the traditional authority on medicine whose theories of the four humors dominated European practice for over a thousand years.</note> who teaches what is certain,
But seeks and loves the tricks of the Rosy Brother, <note>original: "Rosei Fratris," referring to a member of the Rosicrucian brotherhood.</note>
And still, through the rules of Theophrastus <note>original: "Theophrasti," referring to Paracelsus (Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim), the Swiss physician who famously burned Galen's books and promoted a new medicine based on alchemy and chemistry.</note> and his *Paragranum*, <note>One of Paracelsus's foundational texts, in which he attacked traditional medical schools and established his own "four pillars" of medicine.</note>
Believes he can become a true Physician:
Through shifting sands, through rocks, through caves, through weapons, through fires,
He marches toward the infernal waters, never to be called back. <note>This is a classical allusion: the author suggests that following Rosicrucian alchemy is a suicidal path to the underworld (the river Styx).</note>

Cease, Rome, to boast so often of your citizens; <note>original: "Quirites," the formal term for Roman citizens. The author is sarcastically comparing the legendary heroes of Rome to the supposed "heroes" of the Rosicrucians.</note>
A greater honor rises from the German <note>original: "Teutonico." The Rosicrucian movement originated in Germany with the publication of the *Fama Fraternitatis* in 1614.</note> pole.
The Rosy Brother—if it be true—is the greatest Hero,
The honor of Mars <note>The Roman god of war.</note> and of Art, the love of Mars and of Art:
The pillar of Justice, the grace of the Graces, <note>original: "Charitum," the three goddesses of charm, beauty, and nature.</note> the jewel of the world,
And Father in heaven, and King of both Soil and Sea. <note>This list of titles mocks the Rosicrucians by attributing to them the qualities of God or a universal emperor.</note>
O foolish men who boast such things of themselves,
And he who thinks these things are true is surely even more a fool.

# INSTRUCTION
# TO FRANCE
## ON THE TRUTH OF
## THE HISTORY OF THE BROTHERS
### of the Rose-Cross. <note>The Rosicrucians (Brothers of the Rose-Cross) were a mysterious secret society whose manifestos, published in Germany in the early 1600s, claimed they possessed secret wisdom and the ability to heal the sick. They sparked a massive intellectual controversy throughout Europe.</note>

> *Those will come who shall judge without offense and without favoritism.* <note>original: "Venient qui sine offensa, sine gratia iudicent."</note>

That the nature of the French is too quick to embrace and follow all kinds of new and ridiculous opinions.

1. *The levity of the French in believing novelties.*
2. *The causes of this promptness.*

...this Roman, quite remarkable for having been honored with a third Consulship <note>The Roman senator Servianus, to whom this letter was addressed.</note>, begins his letter with the judgment he gives of this country: *Egypt, which you praise to me, Servianus, I have found to be entirely fickle, wavering, and fluttering at every whim of rumor.* <note>original: "Ægyptum quam mihi laudas Seruiane, totam didici leuem, pendulā, & ad omnia famæ momenta volitantem."</note> Forgive me, Gentlemen, if by applying this passage to France, I seem to wish to censure your actions, and from the very first lines of this discourse accuse you of levity. It is not I who dare to infringe upon your prudence, or who seem to desire a bit more restraint in your conduct: Julius Caesar was the first to take that leap, remarking quite appropriately in his *Commentaries* that it was the custom of your ancient fathers to stop travelers in crowds, to question pilgrims, and even to compel merchants to declare to them what news was happening in the countries from which they had most recently arrived, so that being informed by their report, *they might enter into deliberations on the most important matters; of which they were bound to repent immediately, since most people gave them fictional answers to suit their desires.* <note>original: "de summis rebus consilia inirent: quorum eos è vestigio pœnitere necesse erat, quum plerique ad voluntatem eorum ficta responderent."</note> Baptista Mantuanus, <note>An Italian Carmelite poet (1447–1516) known for his Latin verse.</note> the Virgil of our recent centuries, after having said many things in praise of the French, makes the same remark about our current customs and ways of doing things:

<margin>In his poem on Saint Dionysius.</margin>
*Capricorn,* he says, *rules over this nation if we believe the stars,* <note>original: "Ægoceros, dit-il, genti dominans si credimus astris," Capricorn was astrologically linked to qualities of instability or sudden shifts in this context.</note>

->*If we grant this power to heaven, it bestows a heart quickly moved,*<-

<note>original: "Si damus hoc cælo arbitrium, cito mobile pectus, / Cordaq́, largitur rerum ſitibunda nouarum." These lines are from the "Astronomica" by the Roman poet Manilius (Book 4), suggesting that human character is shaped by the stars.</note>

2. It would be a lengthy task for me, and perhaps tedious for you, to spend time on the curious search and deduction one might make regarding the cause of these sudden and subtle impulses. These traits, noted as being particular to the French, make them the laughingstock of their neighbors and cause them to be suspected of actually sharing some grain of madness. Countless slanderers have wished to subject them to this reputation through the etymology of their name, which they strive to derive from a certain river in Phrygia <note>An ancient region in what is now central Turkey. In the early modern period, there was a popular myth that the French were descended from Trojan refugees (who were Phrygian).</note>, whose water caused all who drank from it to suddenly become fanatics and insane. I would, however, rightly consider myself a slave to that same madness if, while admitting this reason as legitimate and valid, I did not rather recognize that our France is stained and tarnished by this reproach through the malignant influences of Capricorn—according to our Poet—or of <term>Aries: The zodiac sign of the Ram (Belier in the original), which astrologers of this era often linked to the geography of France</term>, according to all the best astronomers, by whom she recognizes herself as the target and object. Or, more truly, it may be that the actions of peoples are governed by their temperament, and this temperament is subject to the nature, diversity, situation, and other conditions of the places they inhabit. Foreigners have nothing else to reproach us for, except that

...like another Theseus <note>In Greek mythology, Theseus navigated the Labyrinth of Crete to slay the Minotaur.</note>, being entangled under other imperfections in this labyrinth of nature, they extricate themselves more easily by means of Ariadne’s thread <note>Ariadne gave Theseus a ball of thread so he could find his way out of the Labyrinth; here, it serves as a metaphor for logic.</note>—I mean the use and practice of reason. This is a reproach which truly I deem so just and reasonable that I much prefer to lend an ear to your excuses, rather than obliging you—by the little faith I would place in them—to a proof which would infallibly be to your disadvantage.

1. Rhetoric. To learn something is delightful. <note>original: "Addiscere aliquid delectabile est."</note>
That which is wonderful is delightful. <note>original: "Quod admirabile est, delectabile est."</note>
3. Rhetoric.

3. You will perhaps tell me that, following the very testimony of Aristotle <note>The author refers to Aristotle’s *Rhetoric* (Book 1, Chapter 11), where the philosopher argues that learning and admiration are naturally pleasant to humans.</note>, it is a pleasant, restorative, and entirely agreeable occupation to spend time learning something—especially when the object of our knowledge provokes us, with a certain contentment, to admire its novelty and the diversity of its wonders (a true miracle of nature), being capable of urging us through a laudable labor toward the perfection of such knowledge. It is true, Gentlemen, and I grant you, that this inquiry into the nature of things—which, by the very fact of their existence, cannot be anything but good—deserves every kind of praise, and is alone capable of immortalizing the memory of those who vow and consecrate the best of their studies to it. But to search carefully...

And although above I have greatly mistrusted the constancy and noble spirit of our French people, I must nevertheless ingenuously confess my belief is not such that—if by surprise they have given some entry to the charms of these new superstitions—they are so stupid and abandoned by their ancient merriment and vivacity of spirit that, once their daydreams are recognized, they would wish to persist in them any longer.

## That there has never been an opinion so absurd that it has not found brains capable of receiving it.

### CHAPTER II.

|---|---|
| 1. *It is dangerous to innovate anything in the sciences.* | 3. *All extravagances reduced to four kinds.* |

| | 5. *Caprices of learned men.* |

<header>OF THE ROSE-CROSS.</header>

## History of the Brothers of the R. C., more absurd than all those preceding, and by what means it became known in France.

### CHAPTER III.

1. The first degree of wisdom: knowing what is false.
2. That there is no opinion so absurd as the history of the Brothers of the Rose-Cross.
3. Their writings are use-
<column-break/>
less, and cannot be understood.
4. First reports of them, along with the reasons for believing them.
5. Publication of their posters in Paris.

---

<margin>
Seneca,
Epistle 45,
Book 6.
</margin>

1. LACTANTIUS <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD), an early Christian author known for his polished Latin style.</note>, the "Cicero of the Theologians," gives us a precept which we should consider. Instead, with a typically French haste, we seek out an infinity of other matters which—as Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (4 BC – 65 AD), the Stoic philosopher.</note> says—"neither harm the ignorant nor help the knowledgeable" <note>original: *nec ignoranti nocent, nec scientem iuvant*</note>. Had we followed his advice, we would have long ago triumphed over an infinity of daydreams and lies, "which even children do not believe, except for those not yet old enough to pay for their baths" <note>original: *quæ nec pueri credunt, nisi qui nondum ære lavantur*. This refers to very young children who were not yet charged the standard copper coin (the *as*) for entry into the public baths.</note>. For this learned Theologian warns us that the first degree of wisdom, the foundation...

<sig>C.</sig>

<summary>This page introduces a critical history of the Rosicrucians (Brothers of the Rose-Cross), arguing that their history is the most absurd of all intellectual follies and beginning with a philosophical call to wisdom through the rejection of falsehood.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucians, Rose-Cross, Lactantius, Seneca, Paris, wisdom, skepticism, history of ideas</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 17, where the author began citing Lactantius on the definition of wisdom. The text transitions from general philosophical principles to a defense of the author's decision to write a critique of the Rosicrucians, comparing his work to famous satires and debunkings of the past.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>INSTRUCTION ON THE BROTHERS</header>

and the foundation of a fortunate conduct, and the path to a perfect and fulfilled happiness, is to know—

<margin>Persius, Satire 5.</margin>

> *What rings true, and what is the veneer of a deceptive tongue:* <note>original: "Quid solidum crepet, & pictæ tectoria lingue." From Persius, *Satires* 5.25. It describes the ability to distinguish genuine character from superficial flattery.</note>

to discern and choose the true from the false, to pick out the diamond from among the cheap glass beads <note>The original French *hapelourdes* refers to counterfeit gems that look valuable but are worthless.</note>, and in a word, not to let oneself be taken in by anything for which one might one day be suspected of too great a fickleness. **The first step of wisdom,** he says, **is to recognize what is false.** <note>original: "Primus sapientiæ gradus, est falsa intelligere." A quote from Lactantius's *Divine Institutes*.</note>

2. Based on this axiom, along with the one I set out for you in the previous chapter—having established them as two very secure foundations for the initial plans of this Instruction—I dare to pursue and continue the building’s construction. I intend to show you through vivid reasoning that if Plutarch <note>A Greek-Roman biographer and philosopher (c. 46–120 AD).</note> and Calcagnini <note>Celio Calcagnini (1479–1541), an Italian humanist scholar.</note> once put pen to paper to show that the Stoics <note>A school of philosophy that emphasized logic and fate; here, the author suggests their paradoxes were seen as absurd by critics.</note> said things a hundred times more unbelievable than the Poets and storytellers of fables; if La Noue <note>François de la Noue (1531–1591), a French soldier and writer.</note> dedicated a chapter of his *Political Discourses* to railing against the *Amadis* <note>*Amadis de Gaul*, a popular but fantastical series of chivalric romances.</note>; if the Spaniard <note>Miguel de Cervantes.</note> published *Don Quixote of La Mancha* to turn us away from reading those old tales and Romances; if Lucian <note>Lucian of Samosata (c. 125–180 AD), a satirist known for mocking tall tales.</note> declaimed against fabulous narrations; in short, if people write every day against Heretics, Astrologers, Chemists <note>In the 17th century, "Chemists" (*Chymistes*) usually referred to Alchemists seeking the philosopher's stone.</note>, Magicians, Charlatans, **and all that ilk** <note>original: "& id genus omne."</note>: one is obliged with even more for—

<meta>Catchword: te [The text continues with "force" on the following page.]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that true wisdom begins with identifying falsehood and justifies his critique of the Rosicrucians by comparing it to famous literary efforts to debunk myths, romances, and superstitions.</summary>

—the same reason to reveal the core of the matter <note>The original French *descouurir la matte* likely refers to exposing the base or impure metal beneath a surface, or uncovering a deception.</note> to you and to unseal your eyes, to make you recognize that the preconceived opinion of these illuminated Brothers of the R.C. and their prodigious effects and miracles—which you foster and allow to take root in the shifting sands of your imaginations—is far from being more plausible than the whims mentioned above, or the precepts of the Stoics, the fictions of Poets, the tales of Fabulists, the lies of the *Amadis* <note>Refers to *Amadis de Gaula*, a famous and often-criticized chivalric romance.</note>, the nonsense of Romances, the ineptitudes of Lucianic narratives <note>Satirical and fantastical tales in the style of Lucian of Samosata.</note>, or the impostures, boastings, superstitions, and ignorances of Chemists <note>In this context, the author refers to Alchemists.</note>, Astrologers, Magicians, and Charlatans. On the contrary, it is totally vain, false, and impossible, possessing no other existence than that of <margin>Book 1, chapter 54</margin> the Abbey of Thélème in Rabelais <note>A fictional "anti-monastery" in François Rabelais’s *Gargantua*, where the only rule was "Do what thou wilt."</note>, the *Lesina* among the Italians <note>The *Compagnia della Lesina* was a popular 16th-century Italian satire about an imaginary society of extreme misers.</note>, the voices of Roscelin <note>Roscelin of Compiègne (c. 1050–1125), a philosopher who argued that universal concepts were merely "vocal sounds" rather than real things.</note>, the universals of Ockham <note>William of Ockham (c. 1287–1347), a scholastic philosopher who argued that universal concepts do not exist outside the human mind.</note>, Buridan’s ass <note>A famous philosophical paradox concerning a donkey placed between two identical piles of hay; unable to choose, it starves.</note>, the angels of the Sadducees <note>A Jewish sect that, according to the New Testament, did not believe in the existence of angels or the resurrection.</note>, More’s *Utopia* <note>The fictional, idealized island described by Sir Thomas More.</note>, fruitful intentions, the vacuum, the infinite, "horseness" <note>original: *equinité*. A technical term in Scholastic philosophy used to discuss the abstract essence of being a horse.</note>, the mountain of gold, the chimera, and the *conceptual being* <note>original: *ens rationis*. A philosophical term for something that exists only in the mind and has no external reality.</note> of the Philosophers. These things being dissimilar to it in this regard: that they are produced like certain briars, brushwood, and thorns, to serve as a hedge for the pleasant and fragrant garden of Philosophy and the speculation of causes, founded upon the flowerbed of Nature—the true Garden of the Hesperides <note>In Greek mythology, a garden at the edge of the world that produced golden apples.</note> and a miniature portrait of Paradise.

<summary>The author dismisses the Rosicrucian movement as a total fiction, comparing it to famous literary satires, philosophical paradoxes, and imaginary concepts. He argues that while these "thorns" of thought exist, they only serve to hedge in the true garden of natural philosophy.</summary>

—terrestrial paradise; where instead of so many delights, these shadowy <note>The original French *tenebrious* implies "dwellers in darkness" or people who prefer obscurity.</note> and inquisitive Brothers of the R.C. <note>The Rosicrucians (Rose Cross).</note>, after having enticed you with their images, figures, specious titles, triangles, and mysterious *Jehovah* <note>original: "Iehouah"</note>,

lure you at last into a thicket of ignorance, or into some labyrinth of confusion, leaving you only with a perpetual regret in your soul for having been so credulous as to put faith in their false faces, and for having drawn from this stagnant mire and stinking sludge that which the most crystalline springs of all antiquity offered to your merits. 3. For I ask you, Gentlemen, after having read their books, leafed through their writings, and deciphered their enigmas, have you ever been able to conceive of their designs, notice their principles, or gain knowledge of any of their conclusions? I believe you would sooner grant me that *a Chimera buzzing in a vacuum can consume second intentions* <note>original: "Chimera in vacuo bombillans, poſſit comedere ſecundas intentiones." —this labor, and that you would much rather be condemned to seek the light in the shadows of Anaxagoras <note>Anaxagoras was a pre-Socratic philosopher who famously claimed that "snow is black" to illustrate that our senses do not perceive the hidden, underlying reality of things.</note>, to instruct yourselves in the ignorance of Socrates <note>Referring to the Socratic paradox: "I know that I know nothing."</note>, to draw the truth from the well of Democritus <note>Democritus famously stated that truth lies at the bottom of a deep well, implying it is difficult to access.</note>, to seek Religion in the tub of Diogenes <note>Diogenes the Cynic lived in a large ceramic jar (often called a tub) to demonstrate his rejection of worldly comforts.</note>, or to acquire prudence through the madness of Empedocles <note>Empedocles was a philosopher who, according to legend, threw himself into Mount Etna to prove he was an immortal god.</note>, than to explain their word-puzzles, parables, figures, and metamorphoses, only to be unable to gather a single rose of erudition from the midst of so many thorns. This is why <margin>In *Policraticus*.</margin> seeing with Salisbury <note>original: "Sarisberiensis", referring to John of Salisbury (c. 1120–1180), the English philosopher and author of the *Policraticus*, a work on government and ethics.</note> that *nothing is more wretched than to toil most in that which benefits one least* <note>original: "nihil est infælicius quàm in eo in quo minimū proficias plurimum laborare"</note>: I have resolved to relieve you of this misery, and to be the first to break the ice, *standing like a solid signpost* <note>original: "truncoq’ simillimus herme," a reference to Juvenal. A "Herm" was a squared stone pillar topped with a head; the author suggests he is standing firm and immovable against error.</note>, opposing all these false daydreams and persuasions, to make you recognize the entire truth, and to lead you imperceptibly to the discovery of this deception,

> *Dire contagions creep through the unwary multitude.* <note>original: "Dira per incautum serpant contagia vulgus."</note>

To accomplish this more easily, I will <margin>Celsus, Book 1.</margin> make use of the axiom of the Physicians, who *say that he will treat a patient correctly whom the first origin of the cause has not deceived* <note>original: "eum rectè curaturum dicunt, quem prima origo causæ non fefellerit," from the Roman medical writer Aulus Cornelius Celsus.</note>: persuading myself that after having dug up, uncovered, and hacked this tree at the root, it will be easy for me to bundle its branches and make of them faggots <note>Bundles of sticks used for fuel.</note>, which will be reduced to ashes as soon as they are...

...warmed by the slightest flame of the fire of truth.

4. It is a common rumor now circulating in all circles <note>original: "compagnies", referring to social or intellectual gatherings.</note>, and which, proud and puffed up by its place of origin, is spreading throughout all of France: that after so many novelties that the age of our recent ancestors has seen born—after another world discovered <note>A reference to the Americas.</note>, the invention of cannons, compasses, new printing types <note>original: "nouueaux characteres"</note>, clocks, and an infinite number of other secrets—and after Religion, Medicine, Astrology, and all other sciences have been greatly altered by a swarm of new opinions; nature, playing its last hand <note>original: "iouänt de fon refte", a gambling metaphor meaning to risk everything left or to make a final great effort.</note> and gathering all its strength in its final decrepit and broken age, wished to show us the epitome of its wonders, the sinew of its power, and the center of all its virtues. It has done this by giving rise to a company of certain "Brothers" <note>Referring to the Rosicrucians.</note>, illuminated by its knowledge, to set them as a paragon of all the wonders that ever were or ever could be—if indeed any others can be hoped for after these. Nature has communicated to them, in one mass and entirety, all the virtues and properties that it had previously distributed individually among all the species of its creatures. This is why one must not wonder if, like a Gyges <note>Gyges of Lydia was a mythological king said to possess a magical ring that granted him invisibility.</note>, they make themselves invisible, or like an Amphion <note>In Greek mythology, Amphion was a son of Zeus whose music was so powerful it could move stones to build the walls of Thebes.</note> unit—

...[stones] and gems to themselves <note>original: "nes & gemmas ad se alliciunt"; likely the end of the Latin phrase for "attracting stones and gems."</note>; like a Janus <note>A Roman god typically depicted with two faces, looking simultaneously into the past and the future.</note>, they judge the past and the future; like a Daedalus <note>The mythical Greek inventor who crafted wings to fly.</note>, they soar into the air and transport themselves from the East to the West, from the South to the North, by one of the minor mechanisms and secrets of their <term>Cabala</term> <note>A term for secret, mystical knowledge, often derived from Jewish traditions but here applied generally to the occult.</note>. Truly, all these discourses are marvelous, these propositions sublime and far from the ordinary; yet they charm many with the sugar of their honeyed words, making it easier for them to swallow the venom of their deceptions.

having recently spread through Germany, some Professors, Physicians, and studious persons of this city had the curiosity to seek out knowledge of it. To expose even to the Germans themselves everything concerning this "fine" fraternity is an easy task; *those things which deceive have nothing solid; a lie is thin; it shines through if you inspect it diligently.* <note>original Latin: "quae decipiunt nihil habent solidi, tenue est mendacium, perlucet si diligenter inspexeris." From Seneca's Epistles, suggesting that falsehoods cannot withstand close scrutiny.</note> There is no need for Alexander’s sword <note>A reference to Alexander the Great cutting the Gordian Knot, symbolizing a bold solution to a complex problem.</note>, the eyes of a Lamia <note>In mythology, the Lamia could remove her eyes and put them back in, often used as a metaphor for keen or supernatural sight.</note>, or some Oedipus <note>The mythological figure who solved the riddle of the Sphinx.</note> to untie, recognize, and explain the truth of it. ## True History of the Brothers of the R.C.
<note>R.C. refers to the Rose-Cross or Rosicrucians.</note>

### CHAPTER IV.

...the springs of his mind, to draw some reason from it, was what we see practiced every day by Nature, which is accustomed to derive the greatness of her works from the smallness of their principles, and from a weak beginning lead them toward the progress of an accomplished perfection. I do not believe she <note>In the 17th century, Nature was almost always personified as a female figure acting under God's direction.</note> can receive this from any other than that **Prime Mover** and great **Celestial Workman** <note>Philosophical terms for God as the ultimate architect of the universe.</note>, who, having perfected and accomplished the two greatest mysteries of His Divinity by a single word, willed that she, whom He had created from so little, should practice this example in the production of all creatures. Indeed, we see the tallest and most spacious oaks of the New World, upon whose bushy branches the Kings of that country establish their palaces and dwellings <note>The author likely refers to early colonial accounts of indigenous peoples in the Americas or the East Indies building dwellings in giant trees.</note>, being produced from a tiny germ; and elephants and whales from an "atom," if one may speak thus of seed, in comparison to their bodies. 

But Nature is not alone in boasting of this grace; the smallest animals, little beasts, and insects sometimes produce miraculous effects by virtue of this privilege. And what men, through fire, iron, and force, have hardly been able to accomplish, **rabbits**, **moles**, **frogs**, **locusts**, **snakes**, and **sparrows** have achieved while seeking their nourishment. ...he acquired knowledge of the Greek and Latin languages, with which being sufficiently well-provided, he left at the age of sixteen. Joining himself with certain magicians, he learned their sciences and lived with them for the space of five years. Once these were completed, he began his travels at twenty-one years of age, firstly to Turkey, where he acquired a portion of his doctrine, and visited a city in Arabia named Damcar <note>Damcar is a legendary city mentioned in the Rosicrucian manifestos, often identified by scholars as Dhamar in Yemen, though here described as a mythical center of wisdom.</note>, which was inhabited solely by philosophers living in a quite extraordinary manner and very well-versed in the knowledge of nature. These men received him as graciously, and with as much courtesy and evidence of friendship, as the Brahmans <note>High-caste priests and teachers of Hindu law and philosophy.</note> had formerly shown to Apollonius of Tyana <note>original: "Thyanee." Apollonius was a 1st-century Greek philosopher and reputed magician whose life was often paralleled with that of Jesus or other legendary sages.</note>. They greeted him by his own name without his having told or revealed it to them, and they revealed to him several things that had occurred in his monastery during the stay of eleven years he had made there. They assured him that they had long awaited him, as the one who was to be the author of a general reformation of the universe; and they shared with him an infinity of their secrets. Being provided with these, he departed after having stayed there for three years, and made his way as far as Barbary <note>A term used by Europeans from the 16th to 19th centuries to refer to the coastal regions of North Africa.</note>, where he saw the city of Fez. Having communicated with the Sages and Cabalists <note>term: Cabalists. Followers of the Kabbalah, a traditional system of Jewish mysticism and esoteric biblical interpretation.</note> who are there in great number, he passed into Spain, from which being driven out...

...because he wished to establish the first foundations of his Restoration <term>Instauration: A grand undertaking to restore or renew knowledge and society, a key concept in 17th-century philosophy.</term> there, he was forced to retire to his native country of Germany. 3. There were four of them at the beginning, and since then they have increased and multiplied to the number of eight. These men, all being celibate <note>original: "vierges," literally "virgins," referring here to their ascetic and unmarried lifestyle.</note>, wished to make themselves known to the world under the name and epithet of their first founder, who was never recognized by any other title than that of Illuminated Brother of the R. C. They held a firm resolution to observe the laws and principal points of the rule he had left them, namely: to practice Medicine charitably and without taking any reward.

To dress according to the fashion of the country in which they find themselves; to meet once every year at the Congregation.

To choose, when the need arises, a successor.

# CONCERNING THE ROSY CROSS. 39

...to show you their sacrifices, without you having to fear or dread the punishment of an Actaeon <note>In Greek mythology, Actaeon was a hunter who was turned into a stag and torn apart by his own hounds after he accidentally saw the goddess Artemis bathing. The author implies the reader can look upon these "secrets" without divine retribution.</note> or the dreams of the Philosopher Numenius <note>Numenius of Apamea was a 2nd-century philosopher; legend says he was punished by the gods for revealing the Eleusinian mysteries.</note>. All the good and evil they can do to us is precisely a flower without fruit, a lightning bolt without thunder, a word without effect, and to put it briefly, *lightning in a glass, a sound in a pot* <note>original: *fulgur in vitro, sonus in cacabo*. This is a disparaging way of saying their wonders are merely cheap stage magic or empty noise.</note>. The brilliance of their promise is not capable of overshadowing the torch of our reason, nor the number of their propositions of astonishing our memory: and the lies woven with such impudence have no sooner crossed the boundaries of modesty to make a breach in our judgment, than they return beaten and battered by truth itself. It is in vain that they prescribe to us a probation of seven years, which consists only in the torture <term>gehenne</term> <note>The word "gehenne" refers to Gehenna, a place of suffering, used here to describe the grueling nature of the required silence.</note> of a perpetual silence, and cry out to us from afar what the Priests were accustomed to proclaim at sacrifices:

> *The Goddess approaches; far, oh far away, stay you who are profane.* <note>original: *Adventante Dea, procul ô procul, este profani.* This is a variation of a line from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, used to warn the uninitiated to stay away from sacred rites.</note>

They shall no more be masters of our response than we have been of their confession. And since it has been so long since they abandoned it, let us show them that they are wrong to persuade us to receive it.

>*Than so many Alexanders, than so many Masters without end;*
<note>original: *Quam Nicolatistæ, Scotistæ, totq; Thomistæ, / Quam tot Alexandri, quā tot sine sine Magistri;* This is a quote from the "Macaronic" (burlesque Latin) poetry of Merlin Cocaye (the pseudonym of Teofilo Folengo). who, having only viewed the sciences by the exterior of their covering, were more capable of giving us a disgust for them, rather than advancing us in their knowledge. They have approached us, they say, with the wind in the sails of this imagined Restoration <term>Instauration</term> <note>The Rosicrucian manifestos promised a "Universal Reformation" or restoration of the world to a state of primordial perfection.</note>, and have been pushed to our shores following the tide of all the Prophecies that had been made concerning it: it was something that, having been promised to us for a long time, we were only waiting for the hour it appeared in our days, to install the partisans of its glory into possession of its wonders, to the confusion of all its enemies. These words, animated by the hope of such a great good, are truly specious <note>meaning they appear attractive or plausible but are actually deceptive.</note>, and would seem capable of astonishing our judgment, if they also took away the boldness to examine them, just as they will give us occasion, through their absurdities, to make them the subject of our laughter, and to utter with the Satiric Poet,

->*Let the Jew Apella believe it,*<-

*Not I.*
<note>original: *Credat hæc Judæus Apella, / Non ego.* A famous line from Horace's *Satires* (1.5.100-101), used to express extreme skepticism toward a miraculous or superstitious claim. The phrase implies "Tell it to someone more gullible than me."</note>

->**INSTRUCTIONS ON THE BROTHERS**<-
<note>The "Brothers" refers to the Brothers of the Rosy Cross, or Rosicrucians.</note>

2. It was one of the principal delusions of that hermit—Philippus Bombastus, Aureolus, Theophrastus, Paracelsus of Hohenheim—who, having emerged from one of the Swiss Cantons, sought to make a name for himself as much by his multitude of titles as by the pernicious novelty of his doctrine. He wished to persuade us that he was merely the precursor to a certain **Elias the Artist**, <note>A legendary figure in alchemical lore, expected to appear at the end of time to reveal all the secrets of nature and transform the world.</note> who was destined to come after him and burst upon the world equipped with the true secret of the transmutation of metals and of all the sciences. This figure was meant to reform the corruption that had crept into them over the course of time. "What is more useful," he says in chapter <term>viii</term> of the book *On Minerals*, "God allows to be revealed; but what is of greater importance"—namely, the transmutation of metals into gold—"remains hidden from the common people until the arrival of Elias the Artist; when he comes..." <note>original Latin: *Quod vtilius est... Deus patefieri sinit, quod autem maioris momenti est, à sçauoir la transmutation des metaux en or, vulgo adhuc latet vsque ad Eliæ Artiste aduentum, quando is venerit*</note> And in the first treatise of the same book, he speaks these very words: "This also is true: nothing is hidden that shall not be revealed; therefore, after me shall come one whose great work <note>original: *magnale*; a Paracelsian term referring to a great mystery or a manifestation of divine power.</note> does not yet live, who will reveal many things." <note>original Latin: *Hoc item verum est, nihil est absconditum quod non sit retegendum, ideo post me veniet cuius magnale nondum viuit, qui multa reuelabit.*</note> To these passages, Alexander Suchten in his book on the secrets of Antimony, and Eucher Cygnee in his response to Nehusius, <note>Henricus Neuhusius was a critic of the Rosicrucians; the author is listing various alchemical and mystical writers who took these prophecies seriously.</note> grant as much faith as they do to truth itself. Guillaume Postel—who possessed a spirit too ambitious to owe anything to another—seeing that Paracelsus had produced an Elias, brought into play his "Mother Joan," the— <note>Guillaume Postel was a 16th-century mystic who claimed a woman named Mother Joan was the "New Eve" and would help redeem the world.</note>

<meta>catchword: the—</meta>

<summary>The author identifies the alchemist Paracelsus as the source of the Rosicrucian belief in a coming "Elias the Artist" who would reveal all scientific secrets. The first principle and foundation of this Congregation of the <term>R. C.</term> <note>The Rosy Cross, or Rosicrucians, a mysterious brotherhood that claimed to possess secret wisdom.</note> is none other than this restoration, *which the brothers, and others before them, declare not only to be in the future, but already begun* <note>original Latin: *quam fratres, & ante illos alij, non modò ad futuram, sed etiam incœptam esse denunciant*</note>: publishing it themselves and declaring themselves its executors on page 32 of their Manifesto: *We promise more gold than the King of Spain brings back from both the Indies. For Europe is pregnant, and will give birth to a robust boy* <note>original Latin: *Plus auri pollicemur quàm Rex Hispaniæ ex utraque India auferat. Europa enim pregnans est, & robustum puerum pariet*</note>, who can be none other than this <term>Elias the Artist</term> <note>A symbolic figure predicted by Paracelsus who would arrive at the end of the world to reveal the ultimate secrets of alchemy and nature.</note> promised by <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>A famous 16th-century physician and alchemist who blended science with mysticism.</note>. There you have it, Gentlemen: the basis of this <term>Brotherhood</term> <note>original: *Confrairie*</note>, the origin of this <term>Manifesto</term>, the cause of so many chimeras, and the hinge upon which so many fantasies turn. If this source is dried up, the streams will run dry; if this root is cut, the branches will wither; if this foundation is undermined, then farewell to the entire edifice. *Here is the proof, here is the leap; this is the work, this is the labor.* <note>original Latin: *Hic Rhodus, hic Saltus, hoc opus hic labor est.* A combination of two famous phrases: the first challenges someone to prove a boast immediately, and the second, from Virgil, emphasizes the great difficulty of the task at hand.</note>

we can say that Eloquence had its Elias <note>The author uses "Elias" here as a metaphor for a peak or representative figure, referencing the "Elias the Artist" mentioned on the previous page—a symbolic savior of science predicted by Paracelsus.</note> in the age of Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman widely considered the greatest master of Latin eloquence.</note>, during which it was in the vigor of its age; that Philosophy had its own in the time of Plato and Aristotle, since by making the whole globe of the Sciences turn upon these two poles, it has reached us through their means. The same can be said of all other periods during which anything whatsoever is observed to flourish and excel. 

But if we take him <note>Meaning the promised Elias the Artist.</note> to be some person or individual endowed with all the virtues and faculties that he <note>Paracelsus.</note> attributes to him, we can say first of all that it is nothing but a pure chimera and monstrous fantasy of his brain. Astrol.*</note></margin> according to the account of Pico della Mirandola <note>A famous Italian Renaissance philosopher who criticized astrology.</note>, was so bold as to announce to us the coming of his Antichrist for the year 1345.

### INSTRUCTION ON THE BROTHERS ###

...who <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the description of a failed prophet from the previous page.</note> had calculated thirty years of Ephemerides <note>Tables predicting the positions of celestial bodies.</note> after the end of the world; of a Savonarola <note>Girolamo Savonarola (1452–1498), a Dominican friar who briefly led Florence and was famous for his prophetic sermons.</note> whose prophecy is daily disproven by the fortunate governance of the Grand Dukes of Florence, which, at the urging of a Soderini <note>Piero Soderini (1450–1522), a statesman of the Florentine Republic.</note>, he so often published in Florence. Likewise:
<margin>Near the end of his summary of revelations. <note>original Latin: *sub finē cōpendij reu. eius.*</note></margin>
*I have preached openly, and I affirm again, by divine inspiration, that whatever Florentine citizen shall attempt, at home or abroad, to usurp the principality for himself in that city, or to violate the latest government, together with his entire house and all those conspiring with him, God shall punish him severely, and shall finally cause him to perish in a pitiable end;* <note>original Latin: *palam prædicaui, rursusq; affirmo, ex diuina inspiratione...*</note> in short, like that which a Guillaume Postel <note>A 16th-century French linguist and mystic known for his eccentric religious theories.</note> so often wished to persuade us regarding his Mother Joan <note>Postel believed a Venetian woman named Mother Joan was a "New Eve" who would lead a spiritual renewal of the world.</note>: all of which, time—the father of truth—declares to have been most falsely put forward and published by their authors.

These examples show us how to make and give the same judgment concerning the future Elias of Paracelsus <note>A symbolic figure predicted by Paracelsus who would supposedly reveal all secrets of nature and alchemy at the end of time.</note>, which, being founded and established on nothing but the caprice of his imaginations, deserves to be believed less than the preceding ones, all of which at least gave us Astrology as a guarantee for their promises and future predictions. Or, more precisely, we can respond, with Penotus <note>Bernard Georges Penot (c. 1519–1617), a physician and alchemist who initially followed Paracelsus but later criticized his work.</note> in his book *On the Medical Denarius* <note>original Latin: *de denario medico*</note>, that Paracelsus, having transcribed and stolen several of his treatises from the works of Lanfranc <note>A 13th-century surgeon and medical author.</note>, Arnauld de Villeneuve, Raymond <note>Referring to Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher and logician to whom many alchemical works were falsely attributed.</note>...

<summary>The author dismisses the Rosicrucian prophecy of "Elias the Artist" by comparing it to the failed historical predictions of Savonarola and Guillaume Postel. He further attacks Paracelsus's credibility by accusing him of plagiarizing medieval authorities like Arnauld de Villeneuve and Raymond Lull.</summary>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>### ON THE ROSE CROSS ###</header>

Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath whose name was frequently attached to later alchemical works.</note>, Rupescissa <note>Johannes de Rupescissa (c. 1310–1366), a Franciscan friar and alchemist known for his prophetic writings.</note>, Harlandus, and Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot, polymath, and occultist.</note>, he <note>Referring to Paracelsus.</note> had also appropriated the writings of a certain German monk, who, according to Crato’s <note>Johann Crato von Krafftheim (1519–1585), a prominent physician to several Holy Roman Emperors.</note> report, had written learnedly of all Chemistry <note>term: *Chymie*—at this time, the word encompassed both practical chemistry and the spiritual/speculative art of alchemy.</note> two hundred years before him; or rather, as *Melchior Adam* <note>A 17th-century German biographer known for his lives of scholars.</note> testifies in his biography, those of a learned Chemist named *Isaac the Hollander*, which, being written in German, he had brought to light and published under his own name. This is why, foreseeing that over time the works of the said Isaac might come to light and suppress his own by the discovery of such manifest larceny, he had the ingenuity to try to persuade us that after him would come an Elias the Artist <note>A messianic figure in alchemical lore, popularized by Paracelsus, who was expected to reveal all hidden secrets of nature at the end of time.</note>, who would show us plainly and openly all the mysterious secrets that he, through his obscure phrases, new words, and synonyms, had hidden from us in the tangled labyrinth and the obscure chaos of all his works. *This very Isaac the Hollander is he,* says Penot <note>Bernard Georges Penot (c. 1519–1617), a French physician and alchemist who was initially a follower of Paracelsus but later became critical of him.</note>, *of whom Paracelsus prophesied, saying: "And after me shall come Elias the Artist, who shall reveal the secrets of things"; Paracelsus foresaw that Isaac's works would finally be made manifest, and would come into the hands of the most learned men.* <note>original Latin: *Hic ipse Isaacus Hollandus ille est... de quo Paracelsus vaticinatus est, dicens: & veniet post me Helias Artista, qui abdita rerum patefaciet: præuidebat Paracelsus Isaaci opera fore tandem manifesta, & peruentura ad manus doctissimorum virorum.*</note> Be that as it may, it is very evident and manifest that this Elias, brought into the world by Paracelsus and raised and nourished by this fraternity <note>The Rosicrucians.</note>, is the sole cause of its ruin and total destruction...

<summary>The author accuses Paracelsus of plagiarizing the works of Isaac the Hollander and other earlier alchemists, claiming he invented the prophecy of "Elias the Artist" to disguise his theft and explain away his intentionally obscure writing style.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Rosicrucian movement, specifically attacking the internal logic of their prophecies. It follows the argument that Paracelsus's predictions of a future "Elias the Artist" create a logical trap for the Rosicrucians: if they claim to be his successors, they cannot yet possess his secrets, but if they claim to already possess those secrets, they invalidate the prophecy they rely on for legitimacy.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>### INSTRUCTION ON THE BROTHERS ###</header>

...destruction of the **Enlightened ones** <note>term: *Illuminez*—While later associated with the 18th-century Illuminati, here the term refers to the "enlightened" Brothers of the Rose Cross.</note>. For I would very much like them to have satisfied this dilemma, which points its horns to shatter them: either they do not place any faith in this **Elias the Artist** <note>A messianic figure in alchemical lore, popularized by Paracelsus, who was expected to reveal all hidden secrets of nature at the end of time.</note> and the prediction of **Paracelsus**, or they take it as the base and foundation of their origin. If the first, they contradict the passage of their *Manifesto* and that of the **Anonymous** author, cited above; if the latter, then either Paracelsus was mistaken in saying that his **Elias the Artist**—who in the judgment of *Heliophilus à Percis Philochemicus* <note>original: "Heliophilus, à Percis Philochemicus"—A pseudonym for Raphael Eglin (1559–1622), a Swiss theologian and alchemist.</note>, in his *Treatise on Elias the Artist* <note>original: "Traicté de Helia Artiſta"</note>, is only to appear in the year 1650—would be the first who would have the secret of the **powder of projection** <note>term: *pouldre de projection*—A substance sought by alchemists to instantly transform base metals into gold or silver.</note>, and an infinity of other wonders; or our Brothers have lied in saying expressly that they possess this secret, and consequently [lied about all else]; for since they know all things, therefore they must know that one. Whichever side they take, they cannot avoid a most manifest falsehood, and consequently the destruction of their entire **Cabal** <note>term: *Cabale*—In this context, it refers to the secret society's hidden system of knowledge or the group itself.</note> following these contradictions.

...which constrains me to respond to the authorities cited against me, such as a Maier, Cygnaeus, Combach, M. <meta>This page continues the author's critique of specific authorities who have defended the Rosicrucians, specifically focusing on Michael Maier.
