<header>OF THE ROSY CROSS.</header>

But to complete my journey entirely, and to ensure I do not stray from the path <note>Original: *l'Ecliptique*, the ecliptic or celestial path of the sun.</note> of this Instruction until I have reached the turning point <note>Original: *tropique*, meaning the limit or goal, continuing the astronomical metaphor.</note> of truth: I prefer to address each person specifically, and to respond to Michael Maier <note>Michael Maier (1568–1622) was a celebrated German physician and counselor to Emperor Rudolf II. He was one of the most prolific defenders of the Rosicrucian Order.</note>, who is the one who has championed them the most. We should not place any faith in what he has said of them in his book titled, **A True Discovery, or the Gifts of Germany first discovered by her and shared with the whole world** <note>Original Latin: *Verum inventum, seu munera Germaniæ ab ipſa primitus reperta, & toti orbi communicata* (1619).</note>. Because the rumor was already spread by their *Manifesto* and *Confession*, he may just as well have treated them as real in order to attribute their primary source to his own nation—just as he made Charlemagne a German. Through gross ignorance or manifest flattery, he did this to establish the Empire of the Fourth Monarchy <note>A reference to the "Four Kingdoms" from the Book of Daniel; Maier argued that the Roman Empire had been transferred to the Germans.</note> there.

No more credit should be given to his other writings titled, **Silence After the Clamors** <note>Original Latin: *Silentium poſt clamores* (1617).</note>, and **Golden Themis concerning the laws of the brotherhood** <note>Original Latin: *Themis aurea de legibus fraternitatis* (1618).</note>. This is because he is a perpetual chronicler and historian of the most soot-stained <term>Chemists</term> <note>Meaning alchemists; at the time, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably, though here with a pejorative tone.</note> and **bellows-blowers** <note>Original: *souffleurs*. A mocking term for alchemists who spent all their time blowing on their furnace bellows in a futile search for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, as he has shown in his books named, **Golden Symbols** <note>Original Latin: *Symbola aurea* (1617).</note>, **Hieroglyphics** <note>Original Latin: *hieroglyphica*.</note>, and an infinity of others all blackened by the smoke of his furnaces. Furthermore, in his book **On the Tree Bird** <note>Original Latin: *De volucri arborea* (1619), a work discussing the "Barnacle Goose," a bird then believed to grow on trees.</note>, he has given us fables as true histories, similar to those told by the people of Poitou regarding Melusine <note>A figure of European folklore, a woman who turns into a serpent from the waist down while bathing.</note> and Geoffrey "Big-Tooth" <note>Geoffroy à la Grand Dent, a legendary figure from the House of Lusignan known for his giant tusk-like tooth.</note>, or those of the Britons regarding King Arthur, the French regarding their Twelve Peers <note>The legendary paladins of Charlemagne's court, such as Roland and Olivier.</note>, and the Irish regarding Hell... <note>Likely a reference to St. Patrick's Purgatory, a famous pilgrimage site in Ireland believed to be an entrance to the afterlife.</note>

<summary>The author dismisses the writings of Michael Maier, accusing him of inventing the Rosicrucians' reality to bolster German national pride and grouping his serious works with alchemical fantasies and popular folktales.</summary>

themselves participants: <note>Continuing the thought from the previous page: the author argues that the Jesuits’ scholarly reputation is too great for them to be considered members of the Rosicrucian group.</note> and even if that were the case, a short word of explanation is enough to deliver them from it. # Response to the evidence brought forward to confirm this Society.

## CHAPTER VII.

*1. Only imposters call themselves Brothers of the R.C.* <note>R.C. stands for the Rose-Cross or Rosicrucians.</note>
*2. The story of a pilgrim who claimed to be one of them.*

<column-break/>

*3. What he did was not enough to make him be regarded as such.*
*4. Refutation of this story by other more remarkable ones.*

<column-break/>

<margin>1.</margin> If the true way to establish any judgment on present affairs is to reflect on those of the past, and by following the outcome of some, to judge the result of others: I believe, Gentlemen, that there will not be much difficulty in persuading you—after such a long catalog of imposters given to us by Master Coras <note>Jean de Coras (1515–1572) was a French jurist who wrote a famous legal account of the Martin Guerre case.</note> in his history of Arnauld du Tilh, <note>Arnauld du Tilh was the famous 16th-century impostor who successfully convinced a village and a wife that he was the returned soldier Martin Guerre.</note> and the narrative you have since seen of a supposed Sebastian, King of Portugal <note>Sebastian I disappeared in battle in 1578; his death without a body led to "Sebastianism," where several pretenders claimed his identity over the following decades.</note>—that if the persuasions of these deceivers could find a place in the too-hasty imagination of some, who, enticed by some appearance of truth, let themselves be carried away by the lies of their persuasions; it is no wonder that in this century, still heated by the fabrication of so many

<margin>in epistle to Nepotian.</margin>
...novelties, some scoundrels—of whom five or six have already been seen hanging in Germany for having practiced their deception under the title of being Brothers of the R.C. <note>Rose-Cross</note>—have had the boldness to profess themselves as belonging to their Company, and by this means impose upon those who, like the citizens of the city named Rhinocura <note>A reference to Rhinocolura, an ancient city where criminals supposedly had their noses cut off; the author is punning on the French idiom "avoir du nez" (to have a nose for something), meaning the victims lacked the intuition to sniff out a fraud.</note>, have shown themselves to have no nose to recognize them. This is especially so since, following the saying of St. Jerome, "nothing is so easy as to deceive the common and unlearned people with a glib tongue, for they admire whatever they do not understand" <note>original Latin: *nihil est tam facile, quàm vilem plebem & indoctam, volubilitate linguæ decipere, quæ quidquid non intelligit, admiratur*</note>. Indeed, even the ingenuity of the architect of all these lies was so piercing in the Preface of his Manifesto as to want to persuade us that one Adam Haselmeyer <note>Adam Haselmeyer (c. 1560–1630) was a real historical figure and Paracelsian who claimed to have seen a Rosicrucian manuscript before the manifestos were published. He was indeed sentenced to the galleys by the Catholic authorities.</note> (condemned for his wrongdoings) had been sent to the galleys because, out of zeal and too great a devotion to this Company, he had put forward some words in its praise. They hoped by this terror of punishment to have a legitimate excuse for not manifesting themselves more openly, and by keeping our spirits always attached to the hope of the fulfillment of their promises, to take away from us the means—once their impossibility is recognized—of saying with the Moral Poet:

<margin>Palingenius book 3 of the Zodiac.</margin>
<note>Marcellus Palingenius Stellatus was the pseudonym of the 16th-century poet who wrote "Zodiacus Vitae," a work often cited by critics of superstition.</note>
> "O Gods, how great is the poverty of mind everywhere!
> How easy is the path to trifles! Why do you believe these things,
> You madmen? Things which can neither be proven by any reason,
> Nor recognized by the senses: why do you fear empty things?"
> <note>original Latin: *O Dij, quam magna est penuria mentis ubique, / In nugas quam prona via est, quid creditis ista, / Inſani? quæ nec poſſunt ratione probari / Ulla, nec ſenſu agnoſci: quid vanatimetis?*</note>

> He who believes easily is also easily deceived. <note>original Latin: *Qui facilis credit, facilis quoque fallitur idem.*</note>

But this fraternity—which attributes to itself the power to command demons, to make themselves visible and invisible, to dispose of all things as they see fit, and to have no other limits to its power than those of its own will—should blush with shame. 2. He assisted in the capacity of a physician at the death prognosis of a certain woman whom he had helped and succored with some remedies; he made a show of having knowledge of languages and many curiosities regarding the knowledge of simples <term>medicinal plants or herbs used in their natural state</term>, and gave some account of this—

...before a crowd of ignorant people who admire them simply because they do not understand them; or they speak in the presence of those who would have the ability to respond, if the freedom to do so were not denied to them by these "Encyclopedic oracles" <note>A sarcastic label for self-proclaimed experts who claim to possess all-encompassing knowledge.</note>, who refuse to be contradicted in their theses and propositions. Confession <note>Referring to the *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), one of the founding manifestos of the Rosicrucians which claimed they possessed secret, miraculous knowledge.</note>; I would respond briefly that the same could be achieved through **Geomancy** <term>Geomancy: A method of divination interpreting patterns made by earth, stones, or sand, often considered a "low" or superstitious art by scholars of this period.</term>, failing which I would further maintain that this single wonder, observed but a single time and by a single instance, is incapable of providing them with proof founded on experience, which is defined by Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher (c. 129–216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> as *the observation of that which has been seen frequently and in the same manner* <note>original: "eius quod saepius & eodem modo visum est, observatio"</note>: unless we wished to argue like Anaxagoras <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher.</note>, who, having seen a stone fall from the clouds, inferred that the Heavens were an old ruin filled with pebbles and plaster. ->OF THE ROSY CROSS.<-

And this man had a wife and children. And I ask you, Gentlemen, what esteem would you have in good conscience for a man who, calling himself a Brother of the **R. C.** <term>R. C.: An abbreviation for the Rose-Cross (Rosicrucians).</term>, had predicted to you ten or twelve years ago that some great misfortune would dog the heels of France, because all the spaces in the Palace of Paris <note>The Palais de la Cité in Paris, which contained the *Grande Salle* featuring statues (effigies) of French kings.</note> for placing the statues of our Kings—which some think were constructed thus by fate—are now all filled? You would judge infallibly by the effect that followed the prediction that its author was truly one of these **illuminated** <term>Illuminated: (French: *illuminez*) A term for those claiming divine or mystical insight, often used pejoratively by skeptics of this period to imply delusion or fraud.</term> Brothers who boast of knowing all things past, present, and to come; and you would firmly believe in the existence of this Company. 

Now, it so happens that **la Noue** <note>François de la Noue (1531–1591), a famous Huguenot commander and philosopher known as "Iron Arm."</note>, a man of war and free from all superstition, had predicted it more than twenty years ago, in the first chapter of his ***Political and Military Discourses***: Judge then if you should not have as high an esteem for him as for any of these **illuminated Brothers**; and by the same token, how great and remarkable is the foolishness of those who believe in them.

<summary>The author argues that the "prophetic" abilities of the Rosicrucians are frauds, noting that the soldier François de la Noue made similar predictions about the French monarchy through reason rather than mysticism. He uses the legend of the filled royal statue niches in Paris to show that such "predictions" are common knowledge or logical observations.</summary>

1. ALTHOUGH I do not doubt that several interpretations more appropriate than mine could be given to 
<margin>Politics, Book 2</margin> this passage from Aristotle: *He who wishes to find pleasure without sadness, let him turn to Philosophy* <note>original Latin: "Qui velit delectari sine tristitia, ad Philosophiam recurrat." This opinion is founded on the alleged possession of the truth, of which he may be recognized as the leader, and in such capacity honored by those who—<note>original Latin: "conscij propriæ ignorantiæ"</note> aware of their own ignorance—

knowledgeable men admire: <note>original Latin: "homines scientes admirantur"</note> and I do not care to seek any other proof for my explanation than the wise Epicurus, who, after having disregarded his nephritic pains <note>nephritic: relating to the kidneys; Epicurus famously suffered from kidney stones but claimed his philosophy provided mental joy that overcame physical agony</note> through the contentment he received from the multitude of his listeners, ingenuously acknowledges that he is indebted for the enjoyment of such a great good only to Philosophy. This discipline is so fertile in its diversity that, after having provided material for 280 sects—all of which were founded and established on the sole communication of beatitude <note>beatitude: supreme blessedness or happiness</note> and the sovereign good of man—it further persuaded Seneca to assure us that, both in this question and in all others that are no less controversial,
<margin>Epistle 33, Book 1</margin>
> Truth lies open to all; it has not yet been fully occupied; much is left even for those yet to come. <note>original Latin: "Patet omnibus veritas, nondum est occupata, multum etiam ex illa futuris relictum est."</note>

2. However, after having maturely considered this saying of Aristotle, I find that he should have rather warned us to seek our contentment in the profession we might make of never following or embracing the truth. For if the diversity of objects is the sole reason for the pleasure we receive daily, and the truth remains always one and invariable, it follows visibly that falsehoods and the most erroneous opinions are alone capable of satisfying us in our research, for they are no sooner born and hatched—

*Let us hasten to those things which will bring us some help;* <note>original Latin: "ad illa quæ nobis aliquam opem sunt latura properemus"</note> I beg your pardon if, to satisfy your curiosity, I offer you a few more of these conjectures, followed by the explanation and primary source of all these falsehoods. I hope you will place as much faith in this explanation as it holds truth and stands apart from lies. 

I believe—and I am persuaded you will agree—that we should rank at the same level of madness, and draw parallels between the extravagances of the author of these two little booklets <note>Referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis* and the *Confessio Fraternitatis*, the two foundational manifestos of the Rosicrucians.</note> and that melancholic and hypochondriac <term>hypochondriac: in the 17th century, "hypochondria" was considered a form of melancholy or mental delusion believed to arise from the "hypochondrium" (the upper abdomen).</term> of the year 1612. This man was so overcome and dominated by a madness acquired through reading Romances and some purported revelations that he addressed a petition to the King. This document was found in the great hall of the Louvre by one of my friends, who picked it up from under his feet while leaving a sermon. He has permitted me to extract this passage so you may judge the rest of the piece, and likewise see the nature of its author's imagination: 

"**Sire**, there humbly supplicate you with joined hands one hundred Knights from the land of your France, who, by divine inspiration and to fulfill His will and the prophecies, have resolved to take to the field, bearing the name of **Jesus**..."

<summary>The author compares the Rosicrucian manifestos to the delusions of a mentally ill man from 1612 who believed he was leading a band of "Knights of Jesus." He argues that both cases arise from a mind corrupted by reading too many romances and mystical fantasies.</summary>

## The absurdities and nonsense found in the articles of this Company, and in Conrard’s <note>Heinrich Khunrath (c. 1560–1605), a German physician and hermetic philosopher whose work "Amphitheatrum Sapientiae Aeternae" is a foundational text of Christian Kabbalah</note> *Amphitheatre*, along with an explanation thereof.

### CHAPTER IX

1. Errors of the Manifesto and the Confession, and by whom they were refuted.
2. Remarks on some of these.
3. Obscurity, a very frequent vice in the writings of this Company, and in

<column-break/>

Conrard in his *Amphitheatre*.
4. The true interpretation of his book.
5. What his design was in it, and why it is so obscure and difficult to understand.

the Confession, which are the only two books that have brought us the first news of this "illuminated" Congregation, it would be very easy to notice in them—not a hundred, but a thousand, or rather a myriad of reveries, lies, impossibilities, contradictions, and other errors of no lesser consequence. The Jesuit Father Robert <note>Possibly a reference to Robert Bellarmine or another Jesuit polemicist of the period</note> has sketched out a good number of these in section 17 of his *Goclenius Heautontimorumenos* <note>original: "Goclenius the Self-Tormentor," a polemical work attacking the philosopher Rudolph Goclenius</note>; and Libavius <note>Andreas Libavius (c. 1550–1616), a German chemist and physician who was one of the first major critics of the Rosicrucian manifestos</note>, in his treatise *On the Harmonious Magical Philosophy of the Brothers of the Rose Cross* <note>original: "De philosophia harmonica magica fratrum de Rosea cruce"</note>, has noted such a quantity of them that it must be confessed he has left nothing for those who would wish to write after him to glean—unless they wished, like Aesop’s Crow <note>A reference to the fable where a bird covers its nakedness with the stolen feathers of others to appear more beautiful than it is</note>, to cover their nakedness with feathers stolen here and there, hide their ignorance under the doctrine of others, and establish the false luster of their credit and reputation upon the vigils and labors of those who—refusing to let the instruments nature gave them to judge all that is inferior to them and to examine the truth of all things grow rusty and moldy—chose instead to immortalize themselves by exercising those faculties, rather than increasing the number of a heap of muddled writers who serve only as scribes and interpreters for the opinions of others.

By the 17th century, modern historians were beginning to reject this as an oversimplification.</note>, refuted and convicted of being false by **Bodin** <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), an influential French jurist and political philosopher who criticized the Four Monarchies theory in his work on historical method.</note> in his *Method*, chapter 6, and **Duret** <note>Claude Duret (c. 1570–1611), a French judge and scholar interested in linguistics and natural history.</note>, chapter 2, concerning the causes of changes and declines that happen to Kingdoms. It is further seen in his claim that the first **Illuminated Brother** <note>The legendary founder of the Rosicrucians, often identified as Father C.R.C. or Christian Rosenkreuz.</note>, who flourished in the year 1390, was very well-versed in the Greek and Latin languages. He failed to consider that Germany at that time was so barbaric and so devoid of knowledge of these languages—and especially of Greek—that, besides the inept and ridiculous etymologies of **Albertus Magnus** <note>A famous 13th-century German scholar and saint; the author suggests his understanding of word origins was flawed due to a lack of Greek.</note>, this epitaph written for one of the most learned and great scholars of that time provides a notable and sufficient testimony of it:

> ->*Here lies our Master,*<-
> ->*Who disputed twice or thrice*<-
> ->*In Barbara and Celarent,* <note>original Latin: "In barbara & celarent." These and the following terms—Fapesmo and Frisesomorum—are mnemonic names for logical syllogisms used in medieval universities.</note><-
> ->*So that all might wonder,*<-
> ->*In Fapesmo and Frisesomorum,*<-
> ->*Pray for their souls.* <note>original Latin: "Orate pro animas eorum." Note the deliberately poor Latin grammar—"animas" instead of "animabus"—used here to mock the low level of education in that era.</note><-

I would not have wanted to put this point forward if I had not seen it confirmed by Duret in his *Treasury of Languages*, who notes that Italy, France, and Germany had little or no knowledge of the Greek language until...

The words Barbara, Celarent, Fapesmo, and Frisesomorum are mnemonic codes used by medieval students to remember types of syllogisms in logic.</note>

They hope to bring back the fruits of discovery and the conquest of some great treasure to crown the hardship and merit of their research. This is why some have imagined that the entire Encyclopedia <term>Encyclopedia: In this period, this refers to the complete circle of human knowledge or all liberal arts, rather than a specific set of books.</term> was hidden like beautiful flowing water beneath the ice of these difficulties. They believe that the entire secret and method for drawing from this spring was to break the ice from above—that is to say, to gain an understanding of its symbols <note>original: "figures."</note>. The explanation of these symbols was supposedly given to us through a tradition and cabala <note>Here, "cabala" refers to a secret or oral tradition passed down through generations of initiates.</note> so easy to understand that our intellect was suddenly illuminated by the light of all the sciences. Thus, like another Pietro d'Abano <note>original: "Aponenis." Pietro d’Abano (c. 1250–1316) was a philosopher and physician who, according to legend, learned all the liberal arts by magical means.</note>, one would be made capable in an instant of everything he could desire to learn...

firstly, the Epilogue, and the seven degrees, with the exposition, to which he will adapt the figures, the first of which shows the labors to obtain the matter; the second, its properties and nature; the third, the true operations comprised within seven bastions, and the false ones surrounding them; the fourth, the effects during said operations; the fifth, the trials and patience during the work; the sixth—which I would place first—the preparation of oneself and of all things; the seventh, eighth, and ninth consist of meditations; and the tenth shows that only the learned and true <term>Artist</term> <note>In alchemical literature, the "Artist" is the practitioner who performs the "Great Work" of transmutation through both laboratory skill and spiritual devotion.</note> understands the contents of said book. their Religion—*wished*, as Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was an early Christian advisor to Constantine known as the "Christian Cicero."</note> says, *to also prove to posterity with what great piety they defended their religions, they actually diminished the authority of those very religions by their testimony* <note>original: *voluerunt... posteris etiam approbare, quanta pietate defenderint religiones, auctoritatem religionum ipſarum, teſtando minuerunt*. From Lactantius's *Divine Institutes*.</note>. It is true that some, based on this saying from Holy Scripture, *By the same measure you use, it will be measured back to you* <note>original: *Qua menſura menſi fueritis, eadem remetietur vobis*. A reference to Matthew 7:2.</note>, might object to me that it is no wonder Father Garasse is tainted by these slanderers with the same calumny with which he tried to tarnish the reputation of the Lord C. <note>Likely referring to the poet Théophile de Viau, whom Garasse relentlessly prosecuted for atheism and "libertine" verse.</note>—envying the glory that the latter harvests in his youth upon the Parnassus <note>In Greek mythology, the mountain home of the Muses; here used as a metaphor for the heights of French poetry.</note> of the French Muses—and to more easily elbow him aside...

<summary>The author laments the "malice of the age," where even defending the Church can accidentally undermine its authority. He specifically addresses the controversies surrounding forbidden books on magic and Father Garasse's attacks on contemporary poets and "free-thinkers."</summary>
<keywords>Council of Trent, forbidden books, magic, Trithemius, Delrio, François Garasse, Atheism, Lactantius, Théophile de Viau, Parnassus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of a specific individual's character and concludes the "Instruction" by dismissing those so stubborn in their errors that they cannot be reached by reason. It follows the author's lament over the "malice" of the age and the difficulty of defending religious integrity.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>OF THE ROSE-CROSS.</header>

...to his good reputation, which truly I am obliged to preserve for him by the testimonies I have seen him give, both by his motto founded on this beautiful passage from the 
<margin>Lactantius, *On the Origin of Error* <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was an Early Christian author; the quoted work is Book 2 of his *Divine Institutes*.</note></margin>
most eloquent of theologians: *So great is the authority of antiquity that it is said to be a crime to inquire into it; thus, it is everywhere believed as if it were known truth* <note>original: "Tanta est authoritas vetustatis, vt in eam inquirere scelus esse dicatur, itaque ei creditur passim tanquam cognite veritati"</note>; as well as by the French translation he made not six weeks ago of the Latin verses that Monsieur Morel <note>Likely Fedéric Morel (1558–1630), a celebrated French classical scholar and royal printer.</note> had composed in honor of the Trinity; and also the honest modesty and restraint that I have always recognized as being very familiar to him in all his conduct. The other monsters whom I do not wish to confront, due to the little hope there is of overcoming them, are those who are so obstinate in their opinions that, following 
<margin>*Academic Questions*, Book 1 <note>A philosophical work by Cicero exploring skepticism and the certainty of knowledge.</note></margin>
the saying of Cicero, *to whatever opinion they have been carried as if by a storm, they cling to it as if to a rock* <note>original: "ad quamcunque opinionem velut tempestate delati, ad eam tanquam ad saxum adhærescunt"</note>, they give me cause to conclude this Instruction with the verses of the Satiric Poet <note>Persius (34–62 AD), a Roman poet known for his difficult and moralizing Satires.</note>,

->*But you will obtain no favorable omen with any incense,*<-
->*That even a tiny half-ounce of right reason might stick in these fools.*<-
<note>original: "Sed nullo thure litabis, / Hæreat in stultis, breuis vt semuncia recti." From Persius, Satire V.</note>

->THE END.<-

<sig>P i</sig>

<summary>The author concludes his defense of a colleague’s reputation and finishes the treatise by dismissing those whose stubbornness makes them immune to reason or traditional authority. He appeals to the weight of antiquity and the modesty of true scholars against the "monsters" of intellectual obstinacy.</summary>
<keywords>Lactantius, Cicero, Persius, Fedéric Morel, antiquity, religious orthodoxy, skepticism, Trinity, Rose-Cross</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's "Instruction" by citing a contemporary authority to debunk the Rosicrucian phenomenon, transitioning from the personal defense of character on page 115 to a broader intellectual rejection of the secret society.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>INSTRUCTION ON THE BROTHERS</header>

Reader, so as not to deprive you of the satisfaction you will receive in seeing the rejection of this Society <note>Referring to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood, which the author views with deep skepticism.</note> confirmed by the judgment of one of those great Personages—who, by the subtlety of their minds elevated above the common man of letters, deserve such deference that all their opinions should be as oracles to us—I did not want to omit sharing with you this passage from a German book that the phoenix and leader <term>Coriphee<gloss>A term derived from the leader of a Greek chorus, here meaning a preeminent intellectual figure.</gloss></term> of this century, the learned and judicious Campanella <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639) was a famous Italian philosopher and theologian known for his works on political theory and utopia.</note>, composed concerning the *Monarchy of Spain*. This passage appeared to me like St. Elmo <note>A reference to St. Elmo's fire, a glowing light seen on ships during storms, which sailors took as a sign of protection; the author uses it here as a metaphor for a "guiding light" appearing amidst the chaos of the printing process.</note> appearing to sailors during a calm—not the calm following a storm, but one amidst the noise and bustle of a printing house—and thus could only be inserted at the end of this last page. It is presented here according to the translation I had made by one of my friends: 

*Indeed, the fact that all of Christendom is filled on every side with such minds is made all too clear by the so-called Brotherhood of the Rose-Cross; for such a phantom has hardly emerged before its fame and its confessions clearly testify in several various places that it is nothing more than the plaything of an overly mischievous spirit. For it offers hope for such a general reformation <note>A "General Reformation" was a primary promise of the Rosicrucian manifestos, which claimed to offer a secret path to renewing all science and religion.</note>, while also touching upon several strange sciences and things that are partly ridiculous and partly unbelievable.*

<summary>The author concludes his work by citing the philosopher Tommaso Campanella to dismiss the Rosicrucian movement as a mischievous intellectual prank rather than a legitimate spiritual reformation. He characterizes the Brotherhood as a "phantom" that offers incredible and ridiculous claims to the gullible.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucians, Tommaso Campanella, Monarchy of Spain, Reformation, skepticism, Brotherhood of the Rose-Cross, 17th-century philosophy</keywords>

*also throughout all countries many learned Fig-*
*ures & good people have allowed themselves to be so bam-*
*boozled <note>The original French "embabouïner" implies being tricked or hoodwinked through flattery or nonsense.</note>, that they offered them their service & good*
*will, & sometimes by taking their name, &*
*sometimes by keeping it silent, they held it as certain*
*that these Brothers (a thing which was never*
*in use among the ancient Pro-*
*phets) could without*
*difficulty divine the name & the place of these voluntary*
*Clients, within the Mirror of Solomon <note>A legendary scrying glass or occult device used for divination or seeing across distances.</note>, or else by*
*some other means; indeed, some among them*
*were so foolish, that they held as a singular &*
*high mystery the reformation of the whole world, im-*
*printed together with the Fame <note>A reference to the "Fama Fraternitatis," the first Rosicrucian manifesto published in 1614.</note>, which is by Tra-*
*jano Boccalini <note>Trajano Boccalini (1556–1613) was an Italian satirist. The Rosicrucians famously included a translated chapter of his "Ragguagli di Parnaso" in their own manifesto.</note>, & translated word for word from his*
*Italian Parnassus, and they have explained it Alchemically <note>original: "Chimiquement." In this era, chemistry and alchemy were often used interchangeably.</note>,*
*as if the science of making gold were enveloped*
*within this narrative, which infallibly*
*never entered the mind of the Author.*
*p. 48. book 2. of the Monarchy of Spain.*

**80. NAUDÉ (G.). Instruction to France on the Truth of the History of the Brothers of the Rose-Cross.** Published by Fr. Julliot 1623; <term>duodecimo<note>A book size where each sheet of paper is folded into twelve leaves, creating a small, portable volume.</note></term> in full <term>calfskin<note>A high-quality leather binding made from the skin of a calf.</note></term>, spine with <term>raised bands<note>The horizontal ridges on the spine where the sewing cords are located.</note></term> decorated with <term>small tools<note>Hand-held brass stamps used by bookbinders to apply gold-leaf decorative patterns.</note></term>, 3 gilt lines framing the covers, <term>inner dentelle<note>A lace-like decorative border gilded on the inside edges of the book covers.</note></term>, <term>gilt edges<note>The edges of the pages are coated in gold leaf to protect against dust and provide a luxurious finish.</note></term>. Binding worn, with the <term>headcap<note>The protective fold of leather at the top of the spine.</note></term> and corners slightly used.

A work written with passion by the famous <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653) was a celebrated French scholar and librarian, most famous for managing the library of Cardinal Mazarin.</note> Naudé, against the <term>Rosicrucians<note>The "Brothers of the Rose-Cross," a mysterious and influential secret society that claimed to possess ancient esoteric wisdom.</note></term>, which, by this very fact, provides details regarding their activities. **Original edition. Rare.**
